The limit point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in {{\,\mathrm{\partial }\,}}A$$\end{document}x∈∂A is included in A, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in {{\,\mathrm{\partial in}\,}}A$$\end{document}x∈∂inA, whereas the limit point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in {{\,\mathrm{\partial }\,}}A$$\end{document}y∈∂A is not included in A. In general, A may include any number of such limit points, including all or none.

Theorem 3.8

([34, Thm. 7]). Let A be s.o. in X. Then A=O ∪ BA=O∪B where (1) O is open in X, (2) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O \cap B = \emptyset $$\end{document}O∩B=∅ and (3) B is nowhere dense.

In general, the converse of Theorem 3.8 is false, i.e., not every nowhere dense set is an acceptable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B={{\,\mathrm{\partial in}\,}}A$$\end{document}B=∂inA for a semi-open set A. The following choice is feasible, though:

Theorem 3.9

([34, Thm. 8]). Let X be a topological space and A=O ∪ BA=O∪B where (1) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O\ne \emptyset $$\end{document}O≠∅ is open in X, (2) A is connected and (3) B' = \emptyset B′=∅ where B'B′ is the derived set [i.e., the set of all limit points] of B. Then A is s.o.

The following definition introduces semi-continuity as a weaker form of continuity, in the sense that a continuous map is also semi-continuous but not necessarily vice versa:

Definition 3.10

(Semi-continuity [34, Def. 4]). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f: X \rightarrow X^*$$\end{document}f:X→X∗ be single-valued (continuity not assumed) where X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^*$$\end{document}X∗ are topological spaces. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f: X \rightarrow X^*$$\end{document}f:X→X∗ is termed semi-continuous (written s.c.) if and only if, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O^*$$\end{document}O∗ open in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^*$$\end{document}X∗, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{-1}(O^*)$$\end{document}f-1(O∗) is semi-open in X.

The following theorem is crucial for characterizing semi-continuous functions:

Theorem 3.11

([34, Thm. 12]). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f: X \rightarrow X^*$$\end{document}f:X→X∗ be a single-valued function, X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^*$$\end{document}X∗ being topological spaces. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f: X \rightarrow X^*$$\end{document}f:X→X∗ is s.c. if and only if for [some open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O^*$$\end{document}O∗ with] \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(p)\in O^*$$\end{document}f(p)∈O∗, there exists an A that is semi-open in X such that p∈ Ap∈A and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(A) \subseteq O^*$$\end{document}f(A)⊆O∗.

Topological characterization of terminating consensus

In this section, we endow the set of admissible executions Σ Σ introduced in Section 2 with the uniform and non-uniform topologies defined in [32]: Indeed, in order to be able to explain the relation between terminating and stabilizing consensus, we will re-state a few core results of this topological characterization of terminating consensus.

p-view topology

Let the p-view distance function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_p$$\end{document}dp on the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C of configurations for every process \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \Pi $$\end{document}p∈Π be defined by2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d_p(C,D) = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 0 & \text {if } C \sim _p D \text { and } p\in {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(C)\cap {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(D),\\ 1 & \text {otherwise}. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dp(C,D)=0ifC∼pDandp∈Ob(C)∩Ob(D),1otherwise.Extending this distance function from configurations to executions according to Lemma 3.2, we define the p-view distance function by3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d_p:\Sigma \times \Sigma \rightarrow \mathbb {R}_+,\quad d_p(\gamma ,\delta ) = 2^{-\inf \{t\ge 0\mid d_p(C^t,D^t) > 0\}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dp:Σ×Σ→R+,dp(γ,δ)=2-inf{t≥0∣dp(Ct,Dt)>0},where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = (C^t)_{t\ge 0}$$\end{document}γ=(Ct)t≥0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta = (D^t)_{t\ge 0}$$\end{document}δ=(Dt)t≥0.

Lemma 4.1 reveals that Eq. (3) defines a pseudometric, which differs from a metric by lacking definiteness: There may be executions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \ne \delta $$\end{document}γ≠δ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_p(\gamma ,\delta ) =0$$\end{document}dp(γ,δ)=0.

Lemma 4.1

(Pseudometric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_p$$\end{document}dp [32, Lem. 4.3]). The p-view distance function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_p$$\end{document}dp is a pseudometric, i.e., it satisfies:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d_p(\gamma ,\gamma )&=0 \\ d_p(\gamma ,\delta )&= d_p(\delta ,\gamma ) \qquad \text{(symmetry) }\\ d_p(\beta ,\delta )&\le d_p(\beta ,\gamma ) + d_p(\gamma ,\delta ) \qquad \text{(triangle } \text{ inequality) } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dp(γ,γ)=0dp(γ,δ)=dp(δ,γ)(symmetry)dp(β,δ)≤dp(β,γ)+dp(γ,δ)(triangleinequality)

Uniform topology

The uniform minimum topology (abbreviated uniform topology) on the set Σ Σ of executions is induced by the distance function4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d_{\textrm{u}}(\gamma , \delta ) = \min _{p\in \Pi } d_p(\gamma ,\delta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}du(γ,δ)=minp∈Πdp(γ,δ).Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{u}}$$\end{document}du does not necessarily satisfy the triangle inequality (nor definiteness): There may be executions with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{p}(\beta ,\gamma )=0$$\end{document}dp(β,γ)=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{q}(\gamma ,\delta )=0$$\end{document}dq(γ,δ)=0 but \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{r}(\beta ,\delta )>0$$\end{document}dr(β,δ)>0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r\in \Pi $$\end{document}r∈Π. Hence, the topology on Σ Σ induced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{u}}$$\end{document}du lacks some of the convenient (separation) properties of metric spaces.

The next lemma shows that the decision function of a protocol that solves uniform consensus is always continuous with respect to the uniform topology. Informally, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V is equipped with the discrete topology, this continuity implies that the decision value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta (\gamma )$$\end{document}Δ(γ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta (\delta )$$\end{document}Δ(δ) of two executions γ γ, δ δ that are sufficiently close to each other (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_u(\gamma ,\delta )$$\end{document}du(γ,δ) is sufficiently small) satisfy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta (\gamma )=\Delta (\delta )$$\end{document}Δ(γ)=Δ(δ).

Lemma 4.2

([32, Lem. 4.4]). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta :\Sigma \rightarrow \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}Δ:Σ→V be the decision function of a uniform consensus protocol. Then, Δ Δ is continuous with respect to the uniform distance function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{u}}$$\end{document}du.

Definition 4.3

(v-valent execution). We call an execution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _v \in \Sigma $$\end{document}γv∈Σ, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}v∈V, v-valent, if it starts from an initial configuration where all processes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \Pi $$\end{document}p∈Π have the same initial value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_p(\gamma _v)=v$$\end{document}Ip(γv)=v.

Theorem 4.4

(Characterization of uniform consensus [32, Thm. 5.2]). Uniform consensus is solvable if and only if there exists a partition of the set Σ Σ of admissible executions into sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}v∈V, such that the following holds: Every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv is a clopen set in Σ Σ with respect to the uniform topology induced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{u}}$$\end{document}du.If execution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Sigma $$\end{document}γ∈Σ is v-valent, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Sigma _v$$\end{document}γ∈Σv.

Every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv is a clopen set in Σ Σ with respect to the uniform topology induced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{u}}$$\end{document}du.

Every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv is a clopen set in Σ Σ with respect to the uniform topology induced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{u}}$$\end{document}du.

If execution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Sigma $$\end{document}γ∈Σ is v-valent, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Sigma _v$$\end{document}γ∈Σv.

If execution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Sigma $$\end{document}γ∈Σ is v-valent, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Sigma _v$$\end{document}γ∈Σv.

Non-uniform topology

Whereas the p-view distance function given by Eq. (3) is also meaningful for non-uniform consensus, this is not the case for the uniform distance function as defined in Eq. (4). The appropriate non-uniform minimum topology (abbreviated non-uniform topology) on the set Σ Σ of executions is induced by the distance function5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d_{\textrm{nu}}(\gamma , \delta ) = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \min _{p\in {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(\gamma )\cap {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(\delta )} d_p(\gamma ,\delta )\\ \qquad \qquad \text {if } {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(\gamma )\cap {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(\delta ) \ne \emptyset ,\\ 1\\ \qquad \qquad \text {if } {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(\gamma )\cap {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(\delta ) = \emptyset . \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dnu(γ,δ)=minp∈Ob(γ)∩Ob(δ)dp(γ,δ)ifOb(γ)∩Ob(δ)≠∅,1ifOb(γ)∩Ob(δ)=∅.Like for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{u}}$$\end{document}du, neither definiteness nor the triangle inequality need to be satisfied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{nu}}$$\end{document}dnu. The resulting non-uniform topology is finer than the uniform topology, however, since the minimum is taken over the smaller set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(\gamma )\cap {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(\delta )\subseteq \Pi $$\end{document}Ob(γ)∩Ob(δ)⊆Π: Indeed, less executions are within the open ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\varepsilon ^{\textrm{nu}}(\gamma )$$\end{document}Bεnu(γ) compared to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\varepsilon ^{\textrm{u}}(\gamma )$$\end{document}Bεu(γ), i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\varepsilon ^{\textrm{nu}}(\gamma ) \subseteq B_\varepsilon ^{\textrm{u}}(\gamma )$$\end{document}Bεnu(γ)⊆Bεu(γ). Conversely, some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \in B_\varepsilon ^{\textrm{u}}(\gamma )$$\end{document}δ∈Bεu(γ) need not satisfy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \in B_\varepsilon ^{\textrm{nu}}(\gamma )$$\end{document}δ∈Bεnu(γ), which implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{u}}(\gamma ,\delta ) \le d_{\textrm{nu}}(\gamma ,\delta )$$\end{document}du(γ,δ)≤dnu(γ,δ). It follows that every decision function that is continuous with respect to the uniform topology is also continuous with respect to the non-uniform topology.

Lemma 4.5

([32, Lem. 4.5]). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta :\Sigma \rightarrow \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}Δ:Σ→V be the decision function of a non-uniform consensus protocol. Then, Δ Δ is continuous with respect to the non-uniform distance function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{nu}}$$\end{document}dnu.

(Characterization of non-uniform consensus [32, Thm. 5.3]). The importance of limit points

Additional results established in [32] reveal that certain limit points play a crucial role for consensus solvability. Indeed, since consensus is a continuous function (Lemma 4.2 and Lemma 4.5), it is not too surprising that consensus is impossible if and only if limit points “between” different decision sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _w$$\end{document}Σw, i.e., joint limit points, are admissible.

Applying the following well-known topological Lemma 4.7 to the findings of Theorem 4.4 (resp. Theorem 4.6) yields the limit-based characterization of consensus solvability given in Corollary 4.8.

Lemma 4.7

(Separation Lemma [39, Lemma 23.12]). If Y is a subspace of X, a separation of Y is a pair of disjoint nonempty sets A and B whose union is Y, neither of which contains a limit point of the other. The space Y is connected if and only if there exists no separation of Y. Moreover, A and B of a separation of Y are clopen in Y.

Theorem 4.8

(Separation-based consensus characterization [32, Thm. 6.4]). Uniform (resp. non-uniform consensus) is solvable in a model if and only if there exists a partition of the set of admissible executions Σ Σ into decision sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} Corollary 4.10

(Fair/unfair consensus characterization [32, Cor. 6.7]). Condition (1) in Corollary 4.8 is equivalent to requiring that the decision sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _w$$\end{document}Σw for w≠ vw≠v neither contain any fair execution nor any pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ,\rho '$$\end{document}ρ,ρ′ of unfair executions.

Illustration of a semi-continous function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:X \rightarrow X^*$$\end{document}f:X→X∗. On the left, there is a semi-open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \cup B \subseteq X$$\end{document}A∪B⊆X consisting of two sets A and B that share a common limit point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in {{\,\mathrm{\partial }\,}}A \cap {{\,\mathrm{\partial }\,}}B$$\end{document}x∈∂A∩∂B, which belongs to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\partial in}\,}}A$$\end{document}∂inA. By contrast, the limit point x is mapped to f(x) ∈ f(A)f(x)∈f(A) lying in some open set V ⊆ f(A)V⊆f(A). Since f is semi-continous, the pre-image U only needs to be semi-open. Note that the image of no open ball centered at x would lie solely in f(A) here.

Illustration of two connected components of the decision sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _0=\Sigma _{\gamma _0}\cup \Sigma _{\gamma _0'}$$\end{document}Σ0=Σγ0∪Σγ0′ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _1=\Sigma _{\gamma _1}\cup \Sigma _{\gamma _1'}$$\end{document}Σ1=Σγ1∪Σγ1′, taken from [32, Fig. 4]. Common limit points of two connected components from different decision sets (like \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{\gamma _0}$$\end{document}Σγ0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{\gamma _1}$$\end{document}Σγ1, marked by × ×) must not be admissible by Corollary 4.8 to guarantee terminating consensus solvability.

Topological characterization of stabilizing consensus

In this section, we will use the non-uniform topology introduced in Section 4 in conjunction with the topological results on semi-open sets and semi-continuous functions developed by Levine in [34] (which we re-stated in Section 3) for characterizing the solvability of stabilizing consensus. Note that, since there is no meaningful uniform variant of Stabilizing Agreement (SA) in Definition 2.2, as already mentioned in Section 2, the uniform topology would not be useful here.

We already noted at the beginning of Section 4 that the continuity of the decision function of terminating consensus makes it mandatory that there is no admissible limit point “between” two different decision sets, recall the illustration in Fig. 3. After all, a continous function cannot map a connected space to a disconnected space (cp. Fig. 2). For example, since all executions are admissible in the lossy-link model, resulting in a connected space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma =\mathcal {C}^\omega $$\end{document}Σ=Cω, it is hence impossible to solve terminating consensus.

Since stabilizing consensus can be solved in the lossy-link model (see Section 7.2.1), its decision function cannot be continuous. Rather, it must be able to map a connected space to a disconnected one. Lemma 5.3 below will reveal that the decision function of a proper stabilizing consensus protocol must be semi-continuous, in the sense of Definition 3.10, and that the induced decision sets must be semi-open, in the sense of Definition 3.3.

Clearly, like in the case of terminating consensus, the decision function of any (correct) stabilizing consensus protocol induces a unique partition of the set of admissible executions into disjoint decision sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma =\bigcup _{v\in \mathcal {V}} \Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σ=⋃v∈VΣv. Proper decision functions will ensure that no decision set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv contains an execution that is not one of its own limit points (but rather a limit point of some other decision set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _w$$\end{document}Σw):

Definition 5.1

(Proper stabilizing consensus). A decision function for solving stabilizing consensus is called proper, iff none of its induced decision sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}v∈V, contains any execution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \not \in \overline{{{\,\textrm{Int}\,}}(\Sigma _v)}$$\end{document}γ∉Int(Σv)¯, unless γ γ is an isolated point in Σ Σ (where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\gamma \}$$\end{document}{γ} is open in Σ Σ).

Note that an isolated point in Σ Σ is also an isolated point in the subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv, but not vice versa. Indeed, properness prohibits the inclusion of an execution β β in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv, which is a limit of some sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _1,\beta _2, \dots \in \Sigma _w$$\end{document}β1,β2,⋯∈Σw, but not a limit of any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1,\alpha _2, \dots \in \Sigma _v$$\end{document}α1,α2,⋯∈Σv. If included in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv nevertheless, this β β would constitute an isolated point in the subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv, but not in Σ Σ, so Definition 5.1 prohibits this inclusion.

Whereas Definition 5.1 restricts the class of stabilizing consensus protocols covered by our topological characterization, we will prove later (Lemma 5.8) that it is easy to turn a decision function Δ 'Δ′ of a non-proper stabilizing consensus protocol into a proper decision function Δ Δ.

We first instantiate Theorem 3.11 in our special context, which exploits the fact that every singleton set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{v\}$$\end{document}{v} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}v∈V is open in the discrete topology.

Corollary 5.2

(Characterization of semi-continuous functions). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta : \Sigma \rightarrow \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}Δ:Σ→V (where Σ Σ is equipped with the non-uniform topology, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V is equipped with the discrete topology) be a single-valued function. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta : \Sigma \rightarrow \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}Δ:Σ→V is semi-continuous if and only if for every execution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Sigma $$\end{document}γ∈Σ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta (\gamma )=v\in \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}Δ(γ)=v∈V, there exists a semi-open set A in Σ Σ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in A$$\end{document}γ∈A and Δ (A)=vΔ(A)=v.

Lemma 5.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta :\Sigma \rightarrow \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}Δ:Σ→V be the decision function of a proper stabilizing consensus protocol. Then, Δ Δ is semi-continuous with respect to the non-uniform distance function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{nu}}$$\end{document}dnu.

Proof

We use the only-if direction (⇐ ⇐) of Corollary 5.2. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v=\Delta ^{-1}(v)$$\end{document}Σv=Δ-1(v), and distinguish 3 cases for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Sigma _v$$\end{document}γ∈Σv when determining the required semi-open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subseteq \Sigma _v$$\end{document}A⊆Σv containing γ γ:

(1) If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in {{\,\textrm{Int}\,}}(\Sigma _v)$$\end{document}γ∈Int(Σv), there must be some k≥ 0k≥0 such that the open ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{2^{-k}}(\gamma )$$\end{document}B2-k(γ) with radius \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{-k}$$\end{document}2-k (for distance function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\textrm{nu}}$$\end{document}dnu) satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{2^{-k}}(\gamma ) \subseteq \Sigma _v$$\end{document}B2-k(γ)⊆Σv. We take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=B_{2^{-k}}(\gamma )$$\end{document}A=B2-k(γ) for Corollary 5.2 in this case, which is obviously open and hence semi-open.

(2) If γ γ is an isolated point in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv, the singleton set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\gamma \}$$\end{document}{γ} is open in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv, so we can safely take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\{\gamma \}$$\end{document}A={γ}, which is again open and hence semi-open.

(3) If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in {{\,\mathrm{\partial in}\,}}\Sigma _v$$\end{document}γ∈∂inΣv is an (included) boundary point and hence a limit point of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv (as Δ Δ is a proper stabilizing consensus decision function), every open ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{2^{-k}}(\gamma )$$\end{document}B2-k(γ), k≥ 0k≥0, also intersects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv in a point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _k$$\end{document}βk different from γ γ. Choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _k \in {{\,\textrm{Int}\,}}(\Sigma _v)$$\end{document}βk∈Int(Σv), there is some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _k\ge 0$$\end{document}ℓk≥0 such that the open ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{2^{-\ell _k}}(\beta _k) \subseteq {{\,\textrm{Int}\,}}(\Sigma _v)$$\end{document}B2-ℓk(βk)⊆Int(Σv). We thus take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\{\gamma \}\cup \bigcup _{k\ge 0}B_{2^{-\ell _k}}(\beta _k)$$\end{document}A={γ}∪⋃k≥0B2-ℓk(βk), which is semi-open as an arbitrary union of (semi-)open sets with limit point γ γ, recall Theorem 3.4.

Applying the only-if direction of Corollary 5.2 proves the asserted semi-continuity of Δ Δ. □ □

By applying Lemma 3.6 to the result of Lemma 5.3 while recalling Definition 5.1, we immediately observe that the boundaries of all decision sets are nowhere dense:

Corollary 5.4

(Nowhere dense boundaries). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta : \Sigma \rightarrow \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}Δ:Σ→V be a proper stabilizing consensus decision function, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _v$$\end{document}Σv, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}v∈V be the corresponding decision sets. Then, the boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\partial }\,}}\Sigma _v$$\end{document}∂Σv, and hence the included boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\partial in}\,}}\Sigma _v$$\end{document}∂inΣv, of every decision set is nowhere dense.

Since the union of the boundaries \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\partial }\,}}\Sigma _v$$\end{document}∂Σv of all decision sets contains the points of discontinuity of Δ Δ, Lemma 5.4 is consistent with the result of the following Lemma 5.5, which is a specialization of [34, Thm. 13]:

Theorem 5.5

(Nowhere dense discontinuities). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta : \Sigma \rightarrow \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}Δ:Σ→V be a proper stabilizing consensus decision function. Then, the set P of points of discontinuity of Δ Δ in Σ Σ is nowhere dense.

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V is equipped with the discrete topology, it has a finite open basis since the singleton sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{v\}$$\end{document}{v}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}v∈V, are open. For every point of discontinuity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in P$$\end{document}γ∈P, there exists an open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_\gamma =\{v\}$$\end{document}Vγ={v} for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \mathcal {V}$$\end{document}v∈V, such that for some open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W \subseteq \Sigma $$\end{document}W⊆Σ it holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in W$$\end{document}γ∈W but \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta (W) \not \subseteq V_\gamma $$\end{document}Δ(W)⊈Vγ. By Corollary 5.2, there exists a semi-open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_\gamma $$\end{document}Aγ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in A_\gamma $$\end{document}γ∈Aγ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta (A_\gamma ) \subseteq \{v\}=V_\gamma $$\end{document}Δ(Aγ)⊆{v}=Vγ. By definition, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_\gamma = O_\gamma \cup B_\gamma $$\end{document}Aγ=Oγ∪Bγ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\gamma \subseteq {{\,\mathrm{\partial }\,}}O_\gamma = \overline{O_\gamma }-O_\gamma $$\end{document}Bγ⊆∂Oγ=Oγ¯-Oγ, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \not \in O_\gamma $$\end{document}γ∉Oγ and hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in B_\gamma $$\end{document}γ∈Bγ. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\partial }\,}}O_\gamma $$\end{document}∂Oγ is nowhere dense by Lemma 3.6, the subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\gamma $$\end{document}Bγ is also nowhere dense. As there are only finitely many different sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_\gamma $$\end{document}Vγ, it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigcup _{\gamma \in P}B_\gamma $$\end{document}⋃γ∈PBγ is a finite union of nowhere dense sets, which is also nowhere dense. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P \subseteq \bigcup _{\gamma \in P}B_\gamma $$\end{document}P⊆⋃γ∈PBγ, this also holds for P. □ □

To simplify the proof of the main result of this section, namely our topological characterization of stabilizing consensus solvability given in Theorem 5.7 below, we provide a technical lemma first: Assuming that some obedient process p executes its i-th computing step at some time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_i\ge 0$$\end{document}ti≥0 in an a priori unknown admissible execution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = (C^t)_{t\ge 0} \in \Sigma $$\end{document}γ=(Ct)t≥0∈Σ of a proper stabilizing consensus protocol, Lemma 5.6 tells something about the set of executions6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D_p(\gamma ,{t_i})&= \bigl \{\delta =(D^t)_{t\ge 0} \in \Sigma \mid (p \in {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(\delta ))\nonumber \\&\qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \wedge \exists s: C^{t_i} \sim _p D^s\bigr \} \nonumber \\&= \bigl \{\delta =(D^t)_{t\ge 0} \in \Sigma \mid (p \in {{\,\textrm{Ob}\,}}(\delta )) \nonumber \\&\qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \wedge \exists s: V_p(C^{t_i})= V_p(D^s)\bigr \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dp(γ,ti)={δ=(Dt)t≥0∈Σ∣(p∈Ob(δ))∧∃s:Cti∼pDs}={δ=(Dt)t≥0∈Σ∣(p∈Ob(δ))∧∃s:Vp(Cti)=Vp(Ds)}.that process p cannot distinguish from the execution γ γ based on its local view:

Lemma 5.6

The set of admissible executions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_p(\gamma ,{t_i})$$\end{document}Dp(γ,ti) defined in Eq. (6) satisfies the following properties: (i)For every i≥ 0i≥0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_p(\gamma ,{t_i})$$\end{document}Dp(γ,ti) can be locally computed by process p.(ii)For every i≥ 0i≥0,
